[MD]  docs\archive\Phase5-Observability-Canary.md
================================================================================
  1: # Phase 5 — Observability + Canary Deployment
  2: 
  3: ## Implementation Status: PARTIAL (75%)
  4: 
  5: **Goal:** Best-effort ClickHouse metrics and registry-driven canary routing. Prometheus instrumentation and Prometheus/Grafana are not implemented.
  6: 
  7: The 75% score is 9 of 12 scoped outcomes: Kubernetes ClickHouse deployment, table schema, doc-ocr metrics, ASR metrics, the canary registry field, canary routing, version pinning, canary verification, and the postmortem. The three not delivered are a Compose ClickHouse service, Prometheus instrumentation, and Prometheus/Grafana deployment.
  8: 
  9: **What's Done:** Kubernetes ClickHouse setup, final-outcome metrics from both workers, and canary routing logic
 10: **What's Missing:** Compose ClickHouse, Prometheus instrumentation, and Grafana
 11: 
 12: ---
 13: 
 14: ## What Was Implemented ✓
 15: 
 16: ### ClickHouse for Request Analytics - COMPLETE
 17: 
 18: - ClickHouse Kubernetes deployment + service
 19: - `request_metrics` table schema (columnar, MergeTree engine)
 20: - Best-effort metric logging from both workers
 21: - One best-effort insert attempt per completed job, on its final outcome; a successful insert creates one row and retries that continue do not create rows
 22: - Metric writes run off the event loop; after an insert failure, each worker skips further attempts for 60 seconds and emits at most one warning per back-off window
 23: - Metrics include: request_id, model_id, model_name, model_version, worker_name, latency_ms, status
 24: - Table DDL: [`clickhouse/init.sql`](../clickhouse/init.sql) (reconstructed; verify against a live table)
 25: 
 26: The Kubernetes manifests deploy the ClickHouse server and Service and mount an init ConfigMap generated from `clickhouse/init.sql`, so `request_metrics` is created automatically on pod startup. Docker Compose worker metrics remain disabled unless `CLICKHOUSE_HOST` and optionally `CLICKHOUSE_PORT` are supplied from the shell. The deployment has no persistent volume, so stored metrics do not survive a pod restart or replacement.
 27: 
 28: Each Redis worker job that reaches a final outcome causes one best-effort insert attempt when `CLICKHOUSE_HOST` is configured. A successful insert produces one row; a failed insert may leave no row. Direct WebSocket inference does not write these metric rows, and the gateway does not write metric rows. Jobs that never reach a final outcome, such as a queued job whose worker never completes it, produce no row. ClickHouse is deployed in Kubernetes only; Docker Compose has no ClickHouse service, and an unset host disables worker metric writes without attempting a connection. Configured clients use a 2-second connect timeout and a 5-second send/receive timeout; a failed write still triggers the existing 60-second per-worker back-off.
 29: 
 30: ### Canary Routing Logic - COMPLETE
 31: 
 32: - `canary_percent` column added to `models` table
 33: - `get_model_with_canary()` function in gateway
 34: - Registry-driven traffic splitting (no K8s selector tricks)
 35: - REST version pinning (`?version=v1` bypasses canary)
 36: - Numeric version comparison: with two or more active versions, the highest is the canary and the lowest is stable; versions in between receive no traffic
 37: - WebSocket inference has no canary routing
 38: - The API-key rate limiter uses a fixed 60-second window
 39: - Tested with v1/v2 split, verified via ClickHouse queries
 40: 
 41: ### Prometheus Metrics Instrumentation - NOT IMPLEMENTED
 42: 
 43: There is no Prometheus client instrumentation or `/metrics` endpoint in the current services.
 44: 
 45: ---
 46: 
 47: ## What Was NOT Implemented ✗
 48: 
 49: ### Prometheus + Grafana - NOT IMPLEMENTED
 50: 
 51: Prometheus instrumentation, Prometheus, and Grafana are not included in the current implementation.
 52: 
 53: ClickHouse remains Kubernetes-only. Docker Compose has no ClickHouse service. With no `CLICKHOUSE_HOST`, metric writes are disabled; if a configured ClickHouse service is unreachable, inference continues and that worker backs off metric inserts for 60 seconds after a failed attempt.
 54: 
 55: ---
 56: 
 57: ## How Canary Works (Implemented)
 58: 
 59: ### 1. Register v2 via API (not kubectl)
 60: 
 61: This registers metadata only; deploy a worker that consumes the selected `worker_name`. REST jobs use `worker_name` and ignore `protocol` and `endpoint`. Those fields are used by direct WebSocket inference, which does not use this canary selector.
 62: 
 63: ```bash
 64: curl -X POST http://gateway/v1/models \
 65:   -H "Authorization: Bearer <admin-key>" \
 66:   -d '{
 67:     "name": "doc-ocr",
 68:     "version": "v2",
 69:     "worker_name": "doc-ocr-worker-v2",
 70:     "canary_percent": 10,
 71:     "protocol": "grpc",
 72:     "endpoint": "doc-ocr-worker-v2.modelmesh.svc.cluster.local:50051"
 73:   }'
 74: ```
 75: 
 76: v1 and v2 coexist as separate rows, same `name`, different `version`.
 77: 
 78: ### 2. Traffic Splitting (Registry-Driven)
 79: 
 80: Gateway's REST `get_model_with_canary()` function:
 81: 
 82: - Client specifies `?version=` → Route to that REST version
 83: - Client omits version → Roll random 1-100
 84:   - ≤ canary_percent → Route to highest version (v2)
 85:   - \> canary_percent → Route to lowest version (v1)
 86: 
 87: The percentage is a probabilistic split over requests, not a guaranteed exact ratio for a small sample. With two or more active versions, the highest numeric version is the canary and the lowest is stable; intermediate versions receive no traffic. WebSocket requests do not use this selector.
 88: 
 89: **Key insight**: This is simpler and more flexible than K8s replica-ratio tricks. Traffic split is a data change, not a deployment.
 90: 
 91: ### 3. Monitor via ClickHouse
 92: 
 93: ```sql
 94: SELECT model_name, model_version,
 95:        count() AS successful_jobs,
 96:        avg(latency_ms) AS avg_latency_ms,
 97:        quantile(0.95)(latency_ms) AS p95_latency_ms
 98: FROM request_metrics
 99: WHERE timestamp > now() - INTERVAL 1 HOUR
100:   AND status = 'success'
101: GROUP BY model_name, model_version;
102: ```
103: 
104: Latency statistics include successful jobs only. Calculate error rate separately across all final rows so errors remain in the denominator:
105: 
106: ```sql
107: SELECT model_name, model_version,
108:        countIf(status = 'error') / count() AS error_rate
109: FROM request_metrics
110: WHERE timestamp > now() - INTERVAL 1 HOUR
111: GROUP BY model_name, model_version;
112: ```
113: 
114: Each Redis worker job that reaches a final outcome triggers one best-effort insert attempt. Direct WebSocket inference is not included. Retries that will continue produce no row until the job succeeds or reaches a terminal failure.
115: 
116: ### 4. Promote or Rollback
117: 
118: - **Promote**: Increase `canary_percent` to 100, disable v1
119: - **Rollback**: Set `canary_percent` to 0. Do not disable v2 as part of rollback.
120: 
121: Both via `PATCH /v1/models/{id}`. Zero code changes.
122: 
123: ## Canary Rollback Guard (Optional Phase 11)
124: 
125: `gateway/canary_guard.py` is a standalone CLI; it is not part of gateway request handling. It checks active model versions using ClickHouse final-outcome metrics. For each model with at least two active versions, the highest numeric version is the canary, the lowest is stable, and intermediate versions are ignored. If the highest version is already at 0%, the guard skips the ClickHouse query and reports `noop`. This keeps the required post-rollback noop run observable.
126: 
127: The guard uses two rollback rules:
128: 
129: - **Starvation:** estimates expected canary completions from stable completions and `canary_percent`. If expected canary traffic meets the minimum and observed canary completions are below 20% of expected, it rolls back. This catches workers that are down and therefore produce no error metrics.
130: - **Error-rate delta:** after both versions meet the minimum sample count, rolls back only when the canary error rate exceeds stable by more than the configured margin. If both versions fail at similar rates, it holds.
131: 
132: Defaults are a 10-minute sliding window, 30 minimum requests per version, and a 0.10 error-rate delta. Without `--apply`, the guard is a dry run and changes nothing. With `--apply`, it only sets the canary's `canary_percent` to 0; it does not disable or delete the canary.
133: 
134: After enabling or raising a canary, wait at least one full guard window before running with `--apply`. Do not schedule `--apply` more often than the window. The guard uses a sliding look-back and has no timestamp for when the canary percentage changed, so a recently enabled healthy canary can initially appear starved.
135: 
136: Limitations:
137: 
138: - Queued jobs assigned to a rolled-back canary remain unprocessed until their payload and status keys expire; polling clients eventually receive 404.
139: - The window is a sliding look-back, not an exact count since the canary started.
140: - When stable traffic is thin, the guard holds rather than rolling back on insufficient data.
141: 
142: ### Phase 11 live demos
143: 
144: Demo A used 90 ASR submissions at a 20% canary split: 73 were assigned to stable `v1` and 17 to the nonexistent `asr-worker-v2`. The dry run observed 73 stable completions and no canary completions, and returned `rollback` with the reason `canary starved: 0 completed vs about 18 expected`. The `--apply` run returned `rollback` with `applied: true`; a registry read confirmed `canary_percent=0`. The next run returned `noop` with `canary already at 0`.
145: 
146: Demo B used 10 **synthetic** `error` rows for `asr:v2`. With 73 successful stable `v1` rows and 10 canary error rows, the dry run returned `rollback` with `canary error rate 1.00 vs stable 0.00`; it did not apply the change. Cleanup reset the canary to 0, disabled the demo v2 model, and removed all 10 synthetic rows (verified count: 0).
147: 
148: ---
149: 
150: ## Bugs Found and Fixed
151: 
152: ### 1. Version-pinning ambiguity
153: 
154: **Problem**: `version: str = "v1"` made "explicit v1" and "no preference" indistinguishable.  
155: **Fix**: Changed to `version: str | None = None`. Only trigger canary when `None`.
156: 
157: ### 2. Missing `supports_streaming` field
158: 
159: **Problem**: `get_model_by_id` and `list_models` didn't include `supports_streaming` in `ModelRecord`.  
160: **Fix**: Added field to all registry functions.
161: 
162: ### 3. ClickHouse authentication
163: 
164: **Problem**: Official image now enforces default-user password.  
165: **Fix**: Local dev uses `CLICKHOUSE_SKIP_USER_SETUP=1` (NOT production-safe).
166: 
167: ### 4. ClickHouse failures do not fail jobs
168: 
169: Metric insert failures are caught and logged. Phase 10 moves the synchronous insert off the event loop and backs off further attempts for 60 seconds after a failure.
170: 
171: ---
172: 
173: ## Verification
174: 
175: ### Canary Split Test
176: 
177: - In this run, 20 requests with no version split 10/10 between v1/v2 at 50% canary; this probabilistic split is not guaranteed for every 20-request sample
178: - 5 requests with `?version=v1` → All landed on v1
179: - ClickHouse query correctly separated metrics by `model_version`
180: 
181: **Note**: v1 and v2 pointed at identical worker (proof of mechanism, not real rollout). Real canary would deploy genuinely different v2 worker.

200: ---
201: 
202: ## Key Design Decisions
203: 
204: **Why ClickHouse?**
205: 
206: - Columnar store built for analytical queries
207: - `GROUP BY model_version` at scale (Postgres not designed for this)
208: - Separate transactional (Postgres) from analytical (ClickHouse) workloads
209: 
210: **Why Registry-Driven Canary?**
211: 
212: - Traffic split via data, not K8s replicas
213: - More flexible (per-model percentages)
214: - Easier to automate (API call, not kubectl)
215: - Works with any orchestrator, not K8s-specific
216: 
217: **Why Best-Effort Metrics?**
218: 
219: - Observability outage should never become functional outage
220: - Inserts run off the event loop, are wrapped in try/except, and back off for 60 seconds after failure
221: - Metrics are best-effort
222: 
223: **Why Skip Prometheus/Grafana?**
224: 
225: - Prometheus instrumentation and the Prometheus/Grafana services are not implemented.
226: - The Compose stack does not include ClickHouse.
227: 
228: ---
229: 
230: ## What Would Change with More Time
231: 
232: 1. **Deploy genuine v2**: Different preprocessing or confidence threshold, not same worker
233: 2. **Add Prometheus + Grafana**: Real-time dashboards and instrumentation (not implemented)
234: 3. **Statistical significance testing**: Don't promote on 1-2 samples, need real volume
235: 4. **Multi-model canary**: Test framework with multiple models simultaneously
236: 
237: ---
238: 
239: ## Files Implemented
240: 
241: - `k8s/clickhouse/deployment.yaml` - ClickHouse Kubernetes deployment
242: - `k8s/clickhouse/service.yaml` - ClickHouse service
243: - `workers/doc_ocr/metrics.py` - Worker metrics with configurable host and failure back-off
244: - `workers/asr/metrics.py` - Worker metrics with configurable host and failure back-off
245: - `gateway/main.py` - Includes `get_model_with_canary()`
246: - `gateway/registry.py` - Added `canary_percent`, `list_versions()`
247: - `gateway/schemas.py` - Updated Pydantic models
248: - `gateway/canary_guard.py` - Optional standalone Phase 11 rollback guard
249: - `tests/test_canary_guard.py` - Guard decision and model-selection tests
250: 
251: ---
252: 
253: ## Production Readiness
254: 
255: **What's currently implemented:**
256: 
257: - Best-effort terminal-job ClickHouse writes from both workers when ClickHouse is available
258: - Canary routing logic
259: - Failure back-off and off-event-loop metric inserts
260: 
261: **What needs work:**
262: 
263: - Prometheus instrumentation and Prometheus + Grafana deployment
264: - Statistical significance checks and alerting for scheduled guard runs
265: - Production secret management for ClickHouse
266: 
267: ---
268: 
269: ## Postmortem Summary
270: 
271: Canary routing is registry-driven. Both workers make one best-effort ClickHouse insert attempt for each completed Redis job; the gateway and direct WebSocket path do not write metrics, and unfinished jobs have no row. ClickHouse is Kubernetes-only, not in Compose. Prometheus instrumentation and Prometheus/Grafana are not implemented.
272: 
273: **Interview talking point**: "Built registry-driven canary routing, so model-version traffic splits are controlled by registry data. Both workers make a best-effort ClickHouse insert attempt for each completed Redis job, recording version, latency, and final status when ClickHouse is available. The gateway and direct WebSocket path do not write metrics, and ClickHouse is not included in Docker Compose. An optional standalone guard detects canary starvation or error-rate regressions and can set the percentage to zero; Prometheus/Grafana are not implemented."
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[MD]  docs\data-stores.md
================================================================================
  1: # Data stores
  2: 
  3: What lives in Postgres, Redis and ClickHouse, with exact keys, lifetimes and the places where the schema and the code disagree. Behaviour marked **verified** was observed on a real Postgres 16 and Redis 7.0.15 in the review environment.
  4: 
  5: | Store               | Holds                                                             | Required?                                          |
  6: | ------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- |
  7: | **Postgres 16**     | Users, API keys, the model registry                               | Yes                                                |
  8: | **Redis 7**         | Job stream, payloads, job status and results, rate-limit counters | Yes                                                |
  9: | **ClickHouse 24.8** | One row per finished job (metrics)                                | No. Kubernetes only, and only if you apply the DDL |
 10: 
 11: ## Postgres
 12: 
 13: ### Schema
 14: 
 15: ```mermaid
 16: erDiagram
 17:     users ||--o{ api_keys : "owns"
 18:     api_keys ||--o{ requests : "unused"
 19:     models ||--o{ requests : "unused"
 20:     users {
 21:         uuid id PK
 22:         text email UK
 23:         text password_hash
 24:         timestamptz created_at
 25:     }
 26:     api_keys {
 27:         uuid id PK
 28:         uuid user_id FK
 29:         text key_hash UK
 30:         int rate_limit_per_min
 31:         bool is_admin
 32:         bool revoked
 33:         timestamptz created_at
 34:     }
 35:     models {
 36:         uuid id PK
 37:         text name
 38:         text version
 39:         text worker_name
 40:         text protocol
 41:         text endpoint
 42:         text status
 43:         bool supports_streaming
 44:         int canary_percent
 45:         text description
 46:         timestamptz created_at
 47:     }
 48:     requests {
 49:         uuid id PK
 50:         uuid api_key_id FK
 51:         uuid model_id FK
 52:         text model_type
 53:         text status
 54:         int latency_ms
 55:         timestamptz created_at
 56:     }
 57: ```
 58: 
 59: | Table      | Notes                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
 60: | ---------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
 61: | `users`    | `password_hash` is bcrypt. Email is unique                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
 62: | `api_keys` | Only the **SHA-256 hex** of the key is stored; the raw key is shown once at creation. `rate_limit_per_min` defaults to 60. `is_admin` and `revoked` default to false. Deleting a user cascades to their keys                                        |
 63: | `models`   | `UNIQUE (name, version)`. Constraints (named): `protocol` in `http`/`grpc`; `status` in `active`/`disabled`/`deleted`; `canary_percent` between 0 and 100 (default 0, not null). `supports_streaming` defaults to false. `version` defaults to `v1` |
 64: | `requests` | **Never written or read by any code.** It exists in `db/init.sql` and the Kubernetes ConfigMap only (checked by searching the whole repository)                                                                                                     |
 65: 
 66: The extension `pgcrypto` is created by `init.sql`; the admin-promotion SQL below relies on its `digest()` function.
 67: 
 68: ### Seed rows
 69: 
 70: `init.sql` inserts two models (`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`):
 71: 
 72: | name      | version | worker_name      | protocol | endpoint                           | supports_streaming |
 73: | --------- | ------- | ---------------- | -------- | ---------------------------------- | ------------------ |
 74: | `doc-ocr` | `v1`    | `doc-ocr-worker` | `http`   | `http://doc-ocr-worker:8001/infer` | false              |
 75: | `asr`     | `v1`    | `asr-worker`     | `http`   | `http://asr-worker:8002/infer`     | true               |
 76: 
 77: These endpoints are Compose service names. In Kubernetes the same names resolve inside the `modelmesh` namespace.
 78: 
 79: ### When the schema is applied
 80: 
 81: - **Compose:** `db/init.sql` is mounted into `/docker-entrypoint-initdb.d/` and runs **only when the Postgres volume is empty**. An existing volume keeps its old schema; apply the files in `migrations/` by hand. They are not mounted.
 82: - **Kubernetes:** the same SQL is embedded in `k8s/postgres/init-configmap.yaml`. The two copies are identical today (checked), but they are two copies; regenerate the ConfigMap from `init.sql` whenever it changes.
 83: 
 84: ### Migrations
 85: 
 86: | File                                                | Does                                                                     | Idempotent? |
 87: | --------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------- |
 88: | `migrations/003_add_supports_streaming.sql`         | Adds `supports_streaming`; sets it true for `asr` v1                     | Yes         |
 89: | `migrations/004_canary_percent_and_constraints.sql` | Adds `canary_percent NOT NULL DEFAULT 0` and the three CHECK constraints | Yes         |
 90: 
 91: Verified: applying both on top of a database built from `init.sql` produced only "already exists, skipping" notices and no errors. A database that predates migration 004 makes `GET /v1/models` fail with `KeyError: 'canary_percent'`.
 92: 
 93: ### Connection settings
 94: 
 95: `DATABASE_URL` (default `postgresql://user:pass@localhost/modelmesh`). The Compose file uses `postgresql://user:pass@postgres/modelmesh` with the password in plain text. Kubernetes reads `db-secret` (`url` and `postgres-password`).
 96: 
 97: ### Making a key an administrator
 98: 
 99: There is **no API** for this. Admin keys are created with SQL (shown in the setup guide):
100: 
101: ```sql
102: UPDATE api_keys SET is_admin = TRUE
103: WHERE key_hash = encode(digest('<the raw API key>', 'sha256'), 'hex');
104: ```
105: 
106: ### Where schema and code disagree
107: 
108: - `status = 'deleted'` is allowed by the constraint, but no code path sets it. `DELETE /v1/models/{id}` sets `disabled`.
109: - `revoked` is read by key lookup, but nothing sets it. There is no endpoint to list or revoke keys.
110: - `requests` is dead schema.
111: 
112: ## Redis
113: 
114: One Redis instance serves the queue, the job state and the rate limiter. Gateway code uses a client with `decode_responses=True`; workers use `decode_responses=False` so payload bytes round-trip.
115: 
116: ### Keys
117: 
118: | Key                           | Written by                             | Lifetime                                          | Content                                                                                                                                             |
119: | ----------------------------- | -------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
120: | `inference_jobs` (stream)     | Gateway; workers on retry              | Trimmed to about `STREAM_MAXLEN` (default 10,000) | One entry per job attempt: `job_id`, `model_id`, `model_name`, `model_version`, `worker_name`, `api_key_id`, `status`, and `retry_count` on retries |
121: | `inference_jobs_dlq` (stream) | Workers                                | Trimmed to about 1,000                            | `job_id`, `reason`, `elapsed_ms`                                                                                                                    |
122: | `payload:{job_id}`            | Gateway                                | `PAYLOAD_TTL_SECONDS` (default 3600)              | The uploaded bytes                                                                                                                                  |
123: | `job:{job_id}:owner`          | Gateway                                | 7200 s (fixed)                                    | The owning **user** ID                                                                                                                              |
124: | `job:{job_id}:status`         | Gateway (queued); workers (processing) | 3600 s (fixed); **deleted** when the job finishes | `{job_id, status, model_name, model_version}`                                                                                                       |
125: | `result:{job_id}`             | Workers                                | 7200 s (fixed)                                    | `{job_id, status: success, result}` or `{job_id, status: failed, error}`                                                                            |
126: | `rl:{api_key_id}`             | Gateway                                | 60 s window                                       | Integer request count                                                                                                                               |
127: 
128: Consumer groups are named `{WORKER_NAME}_group` (for example `doc-ocr-worker_group`). A group is created on first start with ID `0`, so a brand-new group reads the whole existing stream.
129: 
130: ### What actually remains after a job (verified)
131: 
132: Measured with the OCR consumer logic against a real Redis, one 1 MB job:
133: 
134: | After enqueue                                                            | After success                                                                                                                                               |
135: | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
136: | `payload` TTL 3600; `owner` TTL 7200; `status` TTL 3600; stream length 1 | `payload` **still present** (TTL 3600); `owner` present (7200); `status` **deleted**; `result` TTL 7200; stream entry **still there** (length 1, pending 0) |
137: 
138: So:
139: 
140: - A finished job's payload occupies Redis for up to an hour. Acknowledging a stream entry does not delete it; only `MAXLEN` trimming does.
141: - A job that fails after all retries leaves **four** stream entries (the original plus one per retry), each acknowledged, plus one DLQ entry and one `result` key.
142: - Everything about a job disappears after about one to two hours; polling afterwards returns `404`.
143: 
144: ### Memory limits and what a full Redis looks like (verified)
145: 
146: The Kubernetes Redis runs with `--maxmemory 200mb --maxmemory-policy noeviction` and **no volume**. With those settings (Redis 7.0.15), sending maximum-size (10 MiB) uploads back to back:
147: 
148: - the first **15** succeeded;
149: - the **16th returned HTTP 500 "Internal Server Error"**: Redis refused the write and the gateway does not catch the error;
150: - `/ready` still returned **200** because the readiness check only runs `PING`.
151: 
152: Memory use at that point was about 181 MB. Smaller uploads fit in proportion. Because payloads linger for an hour, this limit is reachable by a single client sending large files. Restarting the Redis pod empties the queue, results and payloads.
153: 
154: ### Stream trimming
155: 
156: `XADD ... MAXLEN ~ N` is approximate. A test with `STREAM_MAXLEN=100` ended with 116 entries (reported in the Phase 9 completion report). Trimming can also remove **unread** entries if a worker falls more than `STREAM_MAXLEN` behind; the consumer logs a warning when the stream passes 80% of the limit.
157: 
158: ### Persistence
159: 
160: Compose keeps a `redisdata` volume. Kubernetes has none. Jobs in flight are not durable in Kubernetes by design of the manifests.
161: 
162: ## ClickHouse
163: 
164: ### Table
165: 
166: `clickhouse/init.sql` (marked in its header as **reconstructed** from the insert statements and documented queries; verify with `SHOW CREATE TABLE` against a live table):
167: 
168: ```sql
169: CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS request_metrics (
170:     timestamp     DateTime DEFAULT now(),
171:     request_id    String,
172:     model_id      String,
173:     model_name    String,
174:     model_version String,
175:     worker_name   String,
176:     status        String,      -- 'success' or 'error'
177:     latency_ms    UInt32
178: ) ENGINE = MergeTree
179: ORDER BY (model_name, model_version, timestamp);
180: ```
181: 
182: No partitioning and no TTL, so the table grows without bound.
183: 
184: ### What writes to it
185: 
186: Both Redis workers, once per job, on the **final** outcome only. A retry that will continue writes nothing. `latency_ms` is the duration of the last attempt, not including earlier attempts or backoff sleeps.
187: 
188: Not measured: direct WebSocket inference, and jobs that never finish.
189: 
190: The writer:
191: 
192: - is disabled when `CLICKHOUSE_HOST` is unset;
193: - creates its client lazily with a 2 s connect and 5 s send/receive timeout;
194: - inserts one row per call, off the event loop;
195: - on any failure drops the client and skips attempts for **60 seconds**; rows for jobs finishing in that window are lost, not buffered;
196: - logs at most one warning per back-off window.
197: 
198: Per-process back-off state means each worker process decides independently.
199: 
200: ### Deploying it (Kubernetes only)
201: 
202: The manifests start the server and a Service but **do not apply the DDL and do not mount a volume**. Apply the table by hand after the pod is available:
203: 
204: ```bash
205: kubectl exec -i -n modelmesh deployment/clickhouse -- clickhouse-client --multiquery < clickhouse/init.sql
206: ```
207: 
208: The development image runs with `CLICKHOUSE_SKIP_USER_SETUP=1`, which removes the default-user password. That is not production-safe. Compose has no ClickHouse service, so metrics are off there.
209: 
210: ### Useful queries
211: 
212: Latency over successful jobs only:
213: 
214: ```sql
215: SELECT model_name, model_version,
216:        count() AS successful_jobs,
217:        avg(latency_ms) AS avg_latency_ms,
218:        quantile(0.95)(latency_ms) AS p95_latency_ms
219: FROM request_metrics
220: WHERE timestamp > now() - INTERVAL 1 HOUR AND status = 'success'
221: GROUP BY model_name, model_version;
222: ```
223: 
224: Error rate over all final rows (errors stay in the denominator):
225: 
226: ```sql
227: SELECT model_name, model_version,
228:        countIf(status = 'error') / count() AS error_rate
229: FROM request_metrics
230: WHERE timestamp > now() - INTERVAL 1 HOUR
231: GROUP BY model_name, model_version;
232: ```
233: 
234: The canary guard runs a parameterised version of the second query; see [Registry and canary](registry-and-canary.md#the-rollback-guard).
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[MD]  docs\getting-started.md
================================================================================
  1: # Getting started
  2: 
  3: From a fresh clone to a first inference, with Docker Compose (the main path) or minikube. Configuration, monitoring and troubleshooting are in [Operations](operations.md).
  4: 
  5: ## Prerequisites
  6: 
  7: | Need                                 | For                                                                                                                           |
  8: | ------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
  9: | Docker with the Compose plugin       | Running the stack                                                                                                             |
 10: | About 10 GB of free disk             | Images: PyTorch (CPU) plus the EasyOCR and Whisper weights, which are **downloaded at image build time**                      |
 11: | `curl` and `jq`                      | The examples and the smoke test                                                                                               |
 12: | Several GiB of free memory           | The OCR and ASR containers each reported about 1.1 to 1.2 GiB at rest (see [Operations](operations.md#resource-sizing))       |
 13: | Python 3.10 or newer on Linux or WSL | Running tests and the WebSocket client. Create the virtual environment on the Linux filesystem, not on a Windows-mounted path |
 14: | minikube and `kubectl`               | The Kubernetes path only                                                                                                      |
 15: 
 16: **Expect the first build to be slow.** On the author's Windows laptop the first `docker compose up --build` took about **51 minutes**. Later starts take seconds.
 17: 
 18: ## Run with Docker Compose
 19: 
 20: ```bash
 21: # 1. Configuration. Compose reads .env automatically.
 22: cp .env.example .env          # if present; otherwise create .env yourself
 23: #    set JWT_SECRET to a long random value; without it Compose uses the
 24: #    insecure placeholder "change-me-in-production"
 25: 
 26: # 2. Build and start everything (gateway, doc-ocr, asr, postgres, redis)
 27: docker compose up --build -d
 28: 
 29: # 3. Wait for readiness (Postgres and Redis reachable)
 30: curl -s http://localhost:8000/ready        # {"status":"ready"}
 31: ```
 32: 
 33: | Task                           | Command                                                                                       |
 34: | ------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
 35: | Start again later              | `docker compose up -d`                                                                        |
 36: | Stop, keep data                | `docker compose stop` / `docker compose start`                                                |
 37: | Remove containers, keep data   | `docker compose down`                                                                         |
 38: | Remove containers **and data** | `docker compose down -v` (deletes the Postgres and Redis volumes; do not do this by accident) |
 39: | Logs                           | `docker compose logs -f gateway`                                                              |
 40: 
 41: What starts:
 42: 
 43: | Service             | Host ports                |
 44: | ------------------- | ------------------------- |
 45: | `gateway`           | 8000                      |
 46: | `doc-ocr-worker`    | 8001 (HTTP), 50051 (gRPC) |
 47: | `asr-worker`        | 8002 (HTTP), 50052 (gRPC) |
 48: | `postgres`, `redis` | not published             |
 49: 
 50: The worker ports are published for convenience and are **unauthenticated**; see [Security](security.md). `db/init.sql` runs only when the Postgres volume is **empty**; if you had an older volume, apply `migrations/*.sql` by hand ([Data stores](data-stores.md#when-the-schema-is-applied)). The ClickHouse metrics store is not part of Compose, so metrics are off.
 51: 
 52: ## Create sample files
 53: 
 54: The smoke test and load test expect two files in `fixtures/`, which is git-ignored and not shipped. This creates them (needs Pillow 10.1 or newer, tested with 10.3):
 55: 
 56: ```bash
 57: mkdir -p fixtures
 58: python - <<'EOF'
 59: from PIL import Image, ImageDraw, ImageFont
 60: img = Image.new("RGB", (900, 200), "white")
 61: ImageDraw.Draw(img).text((30, 60), "HELLO MODELMESH 12345", fill="black",
 62:                          font=ImageFont.load_default(size=56))
 63: img.save("fixtures/sample_document.jpg")
 64: import wave
 65: with wave.open("fixtures/sample_audio.wav", "wb") as w:
 66:     w.setnchannels(1); w.setsampwidth(2); w.setframerate(16000)
 67:     w.writeframes(b"\x00\x00" * 16000 * 2)      # 2 s of silence
 68: EOF
 69: ```
 70: 
 71: The audio is silence, so ASR returns empty text: it tests the plumbing, not transcription quality. Use a short real speech `.wav` if you want to see a transcript.
 72: 
 73: ## Your first requests
 74: 
 75: ```bash
 76: BASE=http://localhost:8000
 77: 
 78: # Register and log in
 79: curl -s -X POST $BASE/v1/auth/register -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
 80:   -d '{"email":"me@example.com","password":"a-long-password"}'
 81: JWT=$(curl -s -X POST $BASE/v1/auth/login -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
 82:   -d '{"email":"me@example.com","password":"a-long-password"}' | jq -r .access_token)
 83: 
 84: # Create an API key (shown once; keep it)
 85: API_KEY=$(curl -s -X POST $BASE/v1/auth/api-keys -H "Authorization: Bearer $JWT" \
 86:   -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"rate_limit_per_min":100}' | jq -r .key)
 87: 
 88: # See the models (no auth needed)
 89: curl -s $BASE/v1/models | jq
 90: 
 91: # Submit a document, then poll the job
 92: JOB=$(curl -s -X POST $BASE/v1/infer/doc-ocr -H "Authorization: Bearer $API_KEY" \
 93:   -F "file=@fixtures/sample_document.jpg" | jq -r .job_id)
 94: sleep 3
 95: curl -s $BASE/v1/jobs/$JOB -H "Authorization: Bearer $API_KEY" | jq
 96: ```
 97: 
 98: Expected shape of the finished job: `{"job_id": "…", "status": "success", "result": {"text": "HELLO MODELMESH 12345", "avg_confidence": …, "line_count": 1}}` (the exact text depends on OCR). Full contract: [API](api.md).
 99: 
100: ### The same over a WebSocket
101: 
102: ```bash
103: pip install websockets
104: python test_ws_client.py "$API_KEY" asr fixtures/sample_audio.wav
105: python test_ws_client.py "$API_KEY" doc-ocr fixtures/sample_document.jpg
106: ```
107: 
108: The client prints each message and `FINAL_RESULT=yes` on success, and exits non-zero on any error or timeout. It connects to `ws://localhost:8000` (change the script for another host).
109: 
110: ## Becoming an admin
111: 
112: Registering, updating or disabling models needs an admin API key. There is **no API** to create one; promote a key in the database. With Compose:
113: 
114: ```bash
115: docker compose exec -T postgres psql -U user -d modelmesh -c \
116:   "UPDATE api_keys SET is_admin = TRUE WHERE key_hash = encode(digest('$API_KEY','sha256'),'hex');"
117: ```
118: 
119: Then `Authorization: Bearer $API_KEY` works on `POST/PATCH/DELETE /v1/models`. Keep admin keys out of shell history, shared logs and command-line arguments.
120: 
121: ## Smoke test
122: 
123: ```bash
124: ./scripts/smoke_test.sh                 # BASE defaults to http://localhost:8000
125: ./scripts/smoke_test.sh --latency 5     # adds a median/p95 section
126: ```
127: 
128: It needs `curl`, `jq`, the fixtures, and Python with `websockets` (it looks for it in `$MODEL_MESH_PYTHON`, `$VIRTUAL_ENV`, `~/.venvs/modelmesh`, `.venv/modelmesh-env`, then `python3`). It checks health and readiness, the model list, auth, an OCR job (the text must contain `HELLO`), an ASR job, both WebSocket paths, the rate limit (a key with limit 2 must give 200, 200, 429), an admin `PATCH` and restore, and prints how long a bad upload takes to fail. The last line is `SMOKE: PASS` or `SMOKE: FAIL (n checks failed)`.
129: 
130: Caveats: the admin check uses `docker compose exec`, so it works **only against a Compose stack**; and with `--latency` it polls every 2 seconds, so recorded times are quantised to about 2-second steps (see [Testing](testing.md#the-other-scripts)).
131: 
132: ## Run on Kubernetes (minikube)
133: 
134: These manifests are written for a local minikube. They have no Ingress, TLS, autoscaling or persistent Redis, and they use development secrets.
135: 
136: ```bash
137: minikube start --cpus=2 --memory=4096
138: minikube addons enable metrics-server
139: 
140: # Build the three images inside minikube's Docker
141: eval $(minikube docker-env)
142: docker build -t modelmesh-gateway:latest -f gateway/Dockerfile .
143: docker build -t modelmesh-doc-ocr:latest -f workers/doc_ocr/Dockerfile .
144: docker build -t modelmesh-asr:latest     -f workers/asr/Dockerfile .
145: 
146: # Apply, in this order
147: kubectl apply -f k8s/namespace.yaml
148: kubectl apply -f k8s/secrets.yaml                 # DB credentials: replace the dev values first
149: kubectl apply -f k8s/redis/
150: kubectl apply -f k8s/postgres/
151: kubectl apply -f k8s/gateway/                     # includes k8s/gateway/jwt-secret.yaml
152: kubectl apply -f k8s/clickhouse/
153: kubectl wait --for=condition=available deployment/clickhouse -n modelmesh --timeout=180s
154: kubectl exec -i -n modelmesh deployment/clickhouse -- clickhouse-client --multiquery < clickhouse/init.sql
155: kubectl apply -f k8s/workers/
156: 
157: kubectl get pods -n modelmesh
158: kubectl port-forward -n modelmesh svc/gateway 8000:8000
159: ```
160: 
161: Notes:
162: 
163: - `k8s/secrets.yaml` and `k8s/gateway/jwt-secret.yaml` are referred to by the setup instructions but were **not in the reviewed export**. Check that they exist and that you have replaced the development values.
164: - The images use `imagePullPolicy: Never`, so `eval $(minikube docker-env)` must be run **before** building.
165: - Minikube was started with `--memory=4096` in the instructions but reported about 7.58 GiB allocatable, so that figure is not a verified 4 GiB limit. The OCR pod needed a 3 GiB limit after 1.5 GiB and 2 GiB were each OOM-killed at start-up.
166: - The Postgres PVC is 1 Gi on minikube's host path. Redis and ClickHouse have **no** volume.
167: - The first worker pods take a while: readiness probes begin after 30 s, and model loading is slow on 2 CPUs.
168: - Admin promotion on Kubernetes uses `kubectl exec` into the Postgres pod with the same SQL as above.
169: 
170: Scale a worker with `kubectl scale deployment doc-ocr-worker -n modelmesh --replicas=3`; replicas share the consumer group.
171: 
172: ## Run the tests
173: 
174: See [Testing](testing.md#running-the-tests). In short: a scratch Postgres database and Redis (a Compose overlay publishes them), `pip install -r tests/requirements.txt`, set `TEST_DATABASE_URL` and `TEST_REDIS_URL`, run `pytest -q`.
175: 
176: ## Load test
177: 
178: `load-test.yml` is an Artillery scenario (5, 20 then 100 requests per second against OCR, ASR and `GET /v1/models`). It reads API keys from `load-test-vars.csv`, which is git-ignored and was not exported; create it with a header `apiKey` and one key per row. With the default rate limit of 60 per minute per key, a run at these rates will mostly measure `429` responses unless the keys have high limits. The only recorded baseline measured gateway **enqueue** latency (p50 2 ms, p95 4 ms) for accepted requests, not end-to-end inference.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[MD]  docs\local-verification-results.md
================================================================================
  1: # Local Verification Results
  2: 
  3: This document summarizes the recorded verification campaigns for ModelMesh. It describes tests and observations, not a production-readiness guarantee. The run reports and raw files are the evidence of record; this summary is not a substitute for them.
  4: 
  5: The campaigns were run on November 22–23, 2026, from commit `d6fd970b76ed5e0a747be709e0f62e7a009d31da`. The worktree was dirty during the runs. See the [verification rules](../verification/RULES.md), [environment record](../verification/00_environment.md), and [consolidated evidence review](../verification/EVIDENCE.md) for scope, provenance, and caveats.
  6: 
  7: > **Evidence availability:** The `verification/` directory is ignored by the current Git configuration. Links to the detailed reports and raw data below resolve in the working copy only. Preserve or distribute that evidence archive alongside this document when reproducibility is required.
  8: 
  9: ## Environment and test baseline
 10: 
 11: The live checks used Windows 11 with Docker Desktop and a local Docker Compose stack. The host had an Intel Core i5-1340P (12 physical / 16 logical cores) and 16,025 MB RAM. Docker Engine was 29.9.2, Docker Compose 5.6.1, Python 3.12.10, PostgreSQL 16.16, and Redis 7.4.12. Compose containers had no CPU or memory limits. The Docker daemon was initially unavailable during environment capture; Docker Desktop was started, and the engine became reachable. WSL2 Docker integration was unavailable at initial capture.
 12: 
 13: The baseline test suite was run three consecutive times against a scratch PostgreSQL database and Redis DB 1:
 14: 
 15: | Runs | Result per run | Warnings per run | Pytest duration: median / p95 / spread | Wall duration: median / p95 / spread |
 16: | ---- | -------------- | ---------------: | -------------------------------------: | -----------------------------------: |
 17: | 3    | 102 passed     |                8 |                   5.36 / 5.92 / 1.55 s |              9.116 / 9.781 / 2.650 s |
 18: 
 19: `pytest --collect-only -q` collected 102 tests in 11 files. Three later runs with complete start/end metadata also each passed all 102 tests with 8 warnings; pytest duration median was 5.65 s, p95 6.01 s, spread 0.56 s. Some earlier test-run wrappers did not record end timestamps. These are local test results, not service-availability measurements. Details and per-file test counts are in the [baseline report](../verification/runs/01_baseline_tests/README.md).
 20: 
 21: ## Source audit
 22: 
 23: The source and Git-history audit found:
 24: 
 25: - Registry lookup queries Postgres on the REST inference and WebSocket request paths; the audit found no in-memory registry cache in the gateway.
 26: - REST inference uses canary selection when no version is pinned. The WebSocket path resolves the requested version directly and does not use canary selection.
 27: - REST queue submission stores the worker name and does not use registry `protocol` or `endpoint`; direct HTTP/gRPC routing by those fields occurs in the WebSocket path.
 28: - Canary selection chooses the highest numeric version as canary and the lowest as stable.
 29: - `inference.proto` declares unary `Predict` and bidirectional streaming `PredictStream`. The gateway receives the WebSocket file as one binary message and sends one final gRPC chunk; this path does not demonstrate incremental upload.
 30: - Both Redis consumers use consumer groups, retry waits of 2, 4, and 8 seconds, a dead-letter stream, and `XAUTOCLAIM` for stale-message reclaim.
 31: - API keys are hashed with SHA-256, passwords use bcrypt, and the rate-limit path uses a transactional Redis pipeline.
 32: - Kafka modules are present but were not found imported or deployed in the application entrypoints. Git history contained implementation commits for retry/reclaim, but no evidence of a measured message-delivery failure rate or before/after measurement.
 33: 
 34: The [static-audit report](../verification/runs/02_static_audit/README.md) contains source locations, quotes, and the exact Git-message search results.
 35: 
 36: ## WebSocket HTTP-versus-gRPC comparison
 37: 
 38: The end-to-end comparison used a full Compose stack and a separate scratch gateway. Five separate processes each performed 20 warm-ups and 100 measured requests per arm and scenario, sequentially, with one WebSocket connection per request. Each arm's latency statistics include successful measured requests only. Confidence intervals bootstrap independent run clusters; the reported Mann–Whitney p-values are request-level and should be interpreted cautiously because requests within a run are not independent.
 39: 
 40: | Payload / model arm               |               HTTP p50 / p95 / spread (n) |              gRPC p50 / p95 / spread (n) |       gRPC − HTTP p50 |        95% run-cluster bootstrap CI (ms / %) |                      Paired runs favoring gRPC |
 41: | --------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------: | ---------------------------------------: | --------------------: | -------------------------------------------: | ---------------------------------------------: |
 42: | Existing image, OCR unary         |         356.45 / 459.37 / 406.13 ms (500) |      350.76 / 470.18 / 3,362.88 ms (500) |     −5.69 ms (−1.60%) |       [−32.37, +30.59] ms / [−8.30%, +9.60%] |                                            3/5 |
 43: | Page-like image, OCR unary        | 27,683.42 / 30,177.53 / 28,518.32 ms (86) | 26,200.42 / 27,907.99 / 4,496.96 ms (15) | −1,483.00 ms (−5.36%) | [−1,483.00, −1,138.05] ms / [−5.36%, −4.31%] | 2/5 paired; only 2/5 runs had paired successes |
 44: | Existing audio, ASR gRPC stream   |         712.01 / 951.54 / 475.78 ms (500) |        698.39 / 940.13 / 769.97 ms (500) |    −13.62 ms (−1.91%) |        [−40.29, +2.60] ms / [−4.96%, +0.36%] |                                            4/5 |
 45: | Existing audio, ASR gRPC unary    |         704.63 / 967.68 / 595.95 ms (500) |        691.97 / 962.52 / 914.64 ms (500) |    −12.66 ms (−1.80%) |       [−25.04, +10.47] ms / [−3.49%, +1.55%] |                                            3/5 |
 46: | Generated speech, ASR gRPC stream |   1,756.68 / 2,437.42 / 2,836.74 ms (500) |  1,743.96 / 2,404.32 / 3,112.30 ms (500) |    −12.72 ms (−0.72%) |      [−24.17, +143.35] ms / [−1.40%, +7.56%] |                                            3/5 |
 47: | Generated speech, ASR gRPC unary  |   2,086.45 / 3,045.83 / 5,158.57 ms (500) |  2,054.86 / 3,113.64 / 3,966.73 ms (500) |    −31.59 ms (−1.51%) |       [−95.75, +28.88] ms / [−4.43%, +1.33%] |                                            3/5 |
 48: 
 49: The existing OCR and ASR fixture comparisons did not establish a measurable p50 difference: their run-cluster confidence intervals include zero. The page-like OCR comparison is not a valid five-run performance comparison: only 134/500 HTTP and 15/500 gRPC measured requests succeeded, and only two run clusters had paired successes. One ASR gRPC-stream capture contained one final response, and the entire audio file was sent in one WebSocket binary message. The optional no-op transport-overhead measurement was **NOT RUN**. See the [full benchmark report](../verification/runs/03_grpc_vs_http/README.md) and its [analysis](../verification/runs/03_grpc_vs_http/analysis.md).
 50: 
 51: ## Queue recovery after worker termination
 52: 
 53: The corrected small-fixture campaign used the 2,113-byte repository OCR image and a separate Compose worker. It completed seven SIGKILL trials: five with `RECLAIM_IDLE_MS=15000`, one with the default `300000`, and one with two successive kills. A separate trial submitted jobs while the worker was down.
 54: 
 55: | Scenario                                            | Jobs accepted | Success | Failed / lost / non-terminal | Distinct job IDs logged processing more than once |
 56: | --------------------------------------------------- | ------------: | ------: | ---------------------------: | ------------------------------------------------: |
 57: | SIGKILL trials, `RECLAIM_IDLE_MS=15000` (5 trials)  |           300 |     300 |                    0 / 0 / 0 |                                                 5 |
 58: | SIGKILL, default `RECLAIM_IDLE_MS=300000` (1 trial) |            60 |      60 |                    0 / 0 / 0 |                                                 1 |
 59: | Two successive SIGKILLs (1 trial)                   |            60 |      60 |                    0 / 0 / 0 |                                                 1 |
 60: | Total SIGKILL trials (7 trials, 8 kill events)      |           420 |     420 |                    0 / 0 / 0 |                                                 7 |
 61: | Jobs submitted while worker was down (1 trial)      |            60 |      60 |                    0 / 0 / 0 |                                                 0 |
 62: 
 63: For the five 15-second reclaim trials, kill-to-last-completion was n=5, median 45.33 s, p95 54.05 s, spread 11.95 s. The default-config trial took 317.88 s and the double-kill trial took 39.30 s from the final kill; both are n=1 observations. Across the eight corrected trials, successful worker processing durations had n=480, median 370.0 ms, p95 690.6 ms, and spread 1,407 ms.
 64: 
 65: Seven IDs appearing in processing logs more than once is evidence of repeated processing attempts, not proof that duplicate results reached callers. The trials do not establish exactly-once processing or a general loss-free guarantee. An earlier series used a 135,371-byte image and is retained separately, but excluded from these primary results. See the [corrected queue report](../verification/runs/13_queue_worker_kill_small/README.md).
 66: 
 67: ## Retry, dead-letter, reclaim, and invalid-input checks
 68: 
 69: The harness loaded both current `consumer.py` files with stubbed inference and metrics, using real Redis DB 1 without loading model weights:
 70: 
 71: - Permanent `ValueError`: one attempt, no delay, failed status retained the specific invalid-input error, one DLQ entry, and zero pending entries.
 72: - Always-transient error: four attempts with waits of 2/4/8 seconds, then failed to the DLQ with zero pending entries. Real wall-clock duration was 14.332 s for OCR and 14.254 s for ASR (one observation each).
 73: - Fails twice, then succeeds: three attempts, 2/4-second waits, success, no DLQ entry, zero pending entries.
 74: - Seeded scenarios: for each worker, 1,500 jobs across three seeds produced 1,377 successes, 123 failures/DLQ entries, 2,102 attempts, and zero jobs left non-terminal or pending. These are synthetic outcomes, not a production failure-rate estimate.
 75: - Reclaim: 20 ghost-consumer entries per worker were reclaimed and succeeded; pending count went from 20 to zero and DLQ remained empty.
 76: 
 77: On the live isolated stack, 10 invalid image uploads and 10 invalid audio uploads all failed with specific errors. OCR submit-to-first-observed-terminal time was n=10, median 61.951 ms, p95 71.079 ms, spread 40.953 ms. ASR was n=10, median 168.972 ms, p95 560.426 ms, spread 644.956 ms. Polling was immediate first, then at 100 ms intervals; these are end-to-end observed job times, not pure worker execution times.
 78: 
 79: A one-sample-per-model pre-split consumer-source overlay produced generic `Processing failed` errors after four logged attempts: OCR 14,117.178 ms and ASR 15,215.839 ms (n=1 each; p95 equals the observation; spread zero). This was not a full historical stack or paired before/after test. The first harness attempt was invalid because its inference stub was not bound into the imported consumers; it is preserved and excluded. Two live setup attempts also failed before submitting jobs (invalid email domain; API-key rate limit above the 1,000/minute schema maximum). See the [retry and invalid-upload report](../verification/runs/15_retry_backoff_dlq_corrected/README.md).
 80: 
 81: ## Enqueue latency, canary selection, and live updates
 82: 
 83: These are local single-machine measurements. The request generator and Docker Desktop shared the Windows host. The benchmark used a 102,400-byte payload, 20 round-robin API keys at the accepted maximum of 1,000 requests/minute, and a dummy worker that consumed no jobs. Each target-rate and path combination ran three separate 60-second runs. All 18 runs returned HTTP 200 for every request; no 401, 422, 429, or 500 responses occurred.
 84: 
 85: | Path         | Target req/s | Pooled n / runs | p50 ms | p95 ms | p99 ms |   max ms | spread ms |
 86: | ------------ | -----------: | --------------: | -----: | -----: | -----: | -------: | --------: |
 87: | Enqueue      |           10 |       1,800 / 3 |  17.81 |  80.87 | 169.06 | 1,107.40 |  1,102.94 |
 88: | Enqueue      |           50 |       9,000 / 3 |  15.35 |  44.90 | 268.90 |   786.87 |    783.06 |
 89: | Enqueue      |          100 |      18,000 / 3 |  22.09 |  76.39 | 218.53 |   751.42 |    746.96 |
 90: | Registry GET |           10 |       1,800 / 3 |   6.79 |  30.51 |  65.17 |   138.79 |    136.59 |
 91: | Registry GET |           50 |       9,000 / 3 |   6.94 |  12.11 |  26.10 |    87.68 |     85.91 |
 92: | Registry GET |          100 |      18,000 / 3 |   8.29 |  23.44 |  72.78 |   321.76 |    319.87 |
 93: 
 94: Peak sampled Redis memory for enqueue was 71,271,872 bytes at 10 req/s, 348,838,048 bytes at 50 req/s, and 689,315,352 bytes at 100 req/s; all stayed below the 900,000,000-byte safety threshold. Redis DB 1 returned to zero keys after each run. After external cleanup, process-wide Redis memory was 41,840 bytes above preflight, so it was close but not byte-identical.
 95: 
 96: Canary allocation was sampled once per configured share with 2,000 unpinned requests. The intervals are 95% Wilson intervals; p-values are Pearson chi-square tests against the configured share.
 97: 
 98: | Versions / setting       |                       Observed canary share | 95% Wilson interval |     Chi-square p |
 99: | ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------: | ------------------: | ---------------: |
100: | v1/v2, 10%               |                          219/2,000 (10.95%) |        9.66%–12.39% |           0.1567 |
101: | v1/v2, 30%               |                          590/2,000 (29.50%) |       27.54%–31.54% |           0.6256 |
102: | v1/v2, 50%               |                          998/2,000 (49.90%) |       47.71%–52.09% |           0.9287 |
103: | v1/v2/v9/v10, 50% on v10 | v10: 1,019 (50.95%); v1: 981; v2/v9: 0 each |  v10: 48.76%–53.14% | 0.3955 for 50/50 |
104: 
105: All 200 explicit pin requests matched the requested version (50 each for v1, v2, v9, and v10); all returned HTTP 200. Zero observed requests to v2 and v9 in the four-version sample do not prove those versions can never be selected.
106: 
107: The live-update loop made 108 HTTP requests at 20.09 req/s: 107 returned 200 and one expected request returned 503 while both test versions were disabled. Each transition below is one observation (n=1; p50 and p95 equal the value; spread zero):
108: 
109: | Change                             | Delay to first observed behavior |
110: | ---------------------------------- | -------------------------------: |
111: | Canary 0 → 100                     |                         15.82 ms |
112: | Disable v2                         |                         42.17 ms |
113: | Disable v1 (both disabled)         |                         52.44 ms |
114: | Re-enable v1                       |                         40.73 ms |
115: | Re-enable v2                       |                         11.68 ms |
116: | WebSocket protocol/endpoint update |                         48.50 ms |
117: 
118: Both gateway containers retained their container IDs during the live updates; the original registry snapshot was unchanged after cleanup. The WebSocket probe latency itself was n=40, median 35.16 ms, p95 44.90 ms, p99 50.01 ms, max 51.61 ms, spread 30.60 ms. These are observed local timings, not a propagation guarantee. Full request distributions, sampled CPU, and raw evidence are in the [registry/canary report](../verification/runs/19_registry_latency_canary/README.md).
119: 
120: ## Failures, skips, and limits
121: 
122: - The historical gRPC and enqueue figures have no saved raw request evidence in the reviewed verification archive; they are not recreated or treated as verified here.
123: - The page-like OCR comparison had severe failures and only two paired run clusters. Five failed requests recorded elapsed values beyond the 90-second receive timeout; the maximum was 17,573,222 ms. A host suspension or clock discontinuity was suspected, not established.
124: - Three run-03 analysis attempts failed (IndentationError, KeyError, TypeError). Earlier generated analysis outputs were overwritten; request-level raw CSVs remain.
125: - The first queue series used the wrong, larger image; it is retained but excluded from the corrected results. The corrected series still recorded seven IDs processed more than once.
126: - Retry run 14 is invalid and excluded. The initial invalid-email and excessive-rate-limit live attempts are retained; neither submitted inference jobs.
127: - Registry pilots 16 and 17 failed during harness execution, and pilot 18 was reduced. They are preserved but excluded from the full run 19 results. Schedule-lag summaries in run 19 included negative medians and should not be used as evidence.
128: - The optional no-op HTTP/gRPC transport benchmark was **NOT RUN**. Kubernetes was **NOT VERIFIED** in the environment pass. Other host workloads were not continuously profiled (**NOT VERIFIED**).
129: - The verification worktree was dirty, and the `verification/` archive is ignored by Git. This document and all measurements describe this local environment; they do not establish production capacity, availability, or correctness under untested workloads.
130: 
131: Detailed commands, run metadata, cleanup checks, per-run records, and preserved failures are available in the linked reports and their `raw/` subdirectories.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[MD]  docs\operations.md
================================================================================
  1: # Operations
  2: 
  3: Configuration reference, sizing, monitoring, routine tasks and troubleshooting. For first-time setup see [Getting started](getting-started.md).
  4: 
  5: ## Configuration reference
  6: 
  7: Every environment variable the code reads (found by searching the whole codebase). Defaults are the values in code.
  8: 
  9: ### Gateway
 10: 
 11: | Variable              | Default                                      | Meaning                                                                                                        |
 12: | --------------------- | -------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
 13: | `DATABASE_URL`        | `postgresql://user:pass@localhost/modelmesh` | Postgres connection. Compose uses `…@postgres/modelmesh`; Kubernetes reads it from `db-secret`                 |
 14: | `REDIS_URL`           | `redis://localhost:6379`                     | Redis connection                                                                                               |
 15: | `JWT_SECRET`          | `change-me-in-production`                    | JWT signing key. If left at the default a warning is logged but the gateway still starts                       |
 16: | `MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES`    | `10485760` (10 MiB)                          | Upload cap for REST and WebSocket; also sets uvicorn's `--ws-max-size` and the gRPC message limit (plus 1 MiB) |
 17: | `PAYLOAD_TTL_SECONDS` | `3600`                                       | Lifetime of `payload:{job_id}`                                                                                 |
 18: | `STREAM_MAXLEN`       | `10000`                                      | Approximate cap of `inference_jobs`                                                                            |
 19: 
 20: ### Workers
 21: 
 22: | Variable           | Default                              | Meaning                                                                        |
 23: | ------------------ | ------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
 24: | `WORKER_NAME`      | `doc-ocr-worker` / `asr-worker`      | Consumer group is `{WORKER_NAME}_group`; must equal the registry `worker_name` |
 25: | `REDIS_URL`        | `redis://redis:6379`                 |                                                                                |
 26: | `RECLAIM_IDLE_MS`  | `300000`                             | How long an entry must be idle before another consumer reclaims it             |
 27: | `STREAM_MAXLEN`    | `10000`                              | Cap applied when a retry re-adds an entry                                      |
 28: | `GRPC_MAX_WORKERS` | `10` (3 in the Kubernetes manifests) | gRPC server threads                                                            |
 29: | `MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES` | `10485760`                           | gRPC message size limit (plus 1 MiB)                                           |
 30: | `WHISPER_MODEL`    | `tiny`                               | ASR only. The image preloads `tiny`; another size would download at start-up   |
 31: | `CLICKHOUSE_HOST`  | unset                                | **Unset disables metrics.** Set to `clickhouse` in the Kubernetes manifests    |
 32: | `CLICKHOUSE_PORT`  | `8123`                               |                                                                                |
 33: | `HOSTNAME`         | `1`                                  | Part of the consumer name; the container runtime sets it                       |
 34: 
 35: `PAYLOAD_TTL_SECONDS` is also set on the workers in Compose and Kubernetes, but **worker code never reads it** (the worker TTLs below are fixed). Setting it there does nothing.
 36: 
 37: ### Canary guard (CLI)
 38: 
 39: `GATEWAY_URL` (default `http://localhost:8000`), `ADMIN_KEY` (required with `--apply`), `CLICKHOUSE_HOST` (default `clickhouse`), `CLICKHOUSE_PORT` (default 8123). See [Registry and canary](registry-and-canary.md#the-rollback-guard).
 40: 
 41: ### Tests
 42: 
 43: `TEST_DATABASE_URL` and `TEST_REDIS_URL`; see [Testing](testing.md).
 44: 
 45: ### Fixed in code (not configurable)
 46: 
 47: | Value                                   | Setting                                       |
 48: | --------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------- |
 49: | Job status TTL / result TTL / owner TTL | 3600 s / 7200 s / 7200 s                      |
 50: | Retries and backoff                     | 3 retries, sleeps of 2, 4, 8 s                |
 51: | Dead-letter stream cap                  | about 1,000 entries                           |
 52: | Consumer read                           | 1 entry, block 5 s                            |
 53: | Maintenance interval                    | 30 s                                          |
 54: | Rate-limit window                       | 60 s                                          |
 55: | JWT lifetime                            | 24 hours                                      |
 56: | Gateway to worker HTTP timeout          | 30 s                                          |
 57: | gRPC timeouts                           | 30 s (unary), 60 s (stream)                   |
 58: | ClickHouse timeouts / back-off          | 2 s connect, 5 s send and receive / 60 s skip |
 59: | Canary guard starvation threshold       | 20% of expected                               |
 60: 
 61: ## Resource sizing
 62: 
 63: ### Reported memory (Docker Compose, Phase 9 report)
 64: 
 65: Measured by the project author with `docker stats` after smoke runs. Small synthetic inputs only; **not** a large-document or load result.
 66: 
 67: | Container      |    Memory |
 68: | -------------- | --------: |
 69: | gateway        |  66.5 MiB |
 70: | doc-ocr worker | 1.185 GiB |
 71: | asr worker     | 1.084 GiB |
 72: | Redis          |  20.0 MiB |
 73: | Postgres       |  31.8 MiB |
 74: 
 75: Each worker loads its model in three processes ([Architecture](architecture.md#worker-container-process-model)), which is why these are large.
 76: 
 77: ### Kubernetes requests and limits (from the manifests)
 78: 
 79: | Workload       | CPU request / limit | Memory request / limit            |
 80: | -------------- | ------------------- | --------------------------------- |
 81: | gateway        | 250m / 500m         | 256Mi / 512Mi                     |
 82: | postgres       | 250m / 500m         | 256Mi / 512Mi                     |
 83: | redis          | 100m / 250m         | 128Mi / 256Mi (`maxmemory 200mb`) |
 84: | clickhouse     | 250m / 500m         | 400Mi / 700Mi                     |
 85: | asr-worker     | 500m / 1 CPU        | 1200Mi / 1800Mi                   |
 86: | doc-ocr-worker | 500m / 1 CPU        | 1Gi / 3Gi                         |
 87: 
 88: Recorded on minikube: the OCR container's cgroup peak was **2,503 MiB**; limits of 1.5 GiB and 2 GiB each caused out-of-memory kills at start-up, so the limit is 3 GiB. Probes: gateway liveness `/health` (5 s initial, 10 s period) and readiness `/ready` (3 s, 5 s); workers readiness `/health` (30 s, 10 s) and liveness `/health` (60 s, 20 s, three failures).
 89: 
 90: Redis at 200 MB with no eviction is easy to fill with large uploads (see [Data stores](data-stores.md#memory-limits-and-what-a-full-redis-looks-like-verified)). Size it for `MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES` times the number of uploads you expect inside one `PAYLOAD_TTL_SECONDS`.
 91: 
 92: ## Monitoring
 93: 
 94: ### Queue
 95: 
 96: ```bash
 97: # Compose
 98: docker compose exec redis redis-cli XLEN inference_jobs
 99: docker compose exec redis redis-cli XPENDING inference_jobs doc-ocr-worker_group
100: docker compose exec redis redis-cli XPENDING inference_jobs asr-worker_group
101: docker compose exec redis redis-cli XREAD COUNT 10 STREAMS inference_jobs_dlq 0
102: 
103: # Kubernetes
104: kubectl exec -n modelmesh deployment/redis -- redis-cli XLEN inference_jobs
105: ```
106: 
107: | Reading                                                      | Means                                                                                                                              |
108: | ------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
109: | `XLEN inference_jobs` close to `STREAM_MAXLEN`               | Entries (including acknowledged ones) are being trimmed; a backlog this long can lose unread jobs                                  |
110: | `XPENDING` count staying above zero with a growing idle time | A consumer took entries and has not finished; they are reclaimed after `RECLAIM_IDLE_MS`                                           |
111: | Entries in `inference_jobs_dlq`                              | Jobs that failed permanently or ran out of retries. The `reason` is the real error. There is **no replay tool**; resubmit manually |
112: 
113: Note that `XLEN` includes acknowledged entries: acknowledging does not delete.
114: 
115: ### Logs
116: 
117: Workers log each job (`Processing job …`, `completed successfully (N ms)`), retries and failures (the OCR worker logs these; the ASR worker does not), and a maintenance line about every 30 seconds with the stream length and pending count. A metrics problem logs `metric insert failed (skipping for 60s)` once per back-off window. The gateway logs through uvicorn, with `api_key=` redacted.
118: 
119: ### Metrics (Kubernetes with ClickHouse only)
120: 
121: Use the queries in [Data stores](data-stores.md#useful-queries). There is no Prometheus, no Grafana and no `/metrics` endpoint, and the health endpoints do not report queue depth or memory.
122: 
123: ### Health
124: 
125: - `/ready` = Postgres and Redis answer. It does **not** notice a full Redis or a missing worker.
126: - `/health` on the gateway and workers always reports ok.
127: - `kubectl top pods -n modelmesh` needs `minikube addons enable metrics-server`.
128: 
129: ## Routine tasks
130: 
131: | Task                   | How                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
132: | ---------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
133: | Add capacity           | Kubernetes: `kubectl scale deployment <worker> -n modelmesh --replicas=N`. Compose publishes fixed host ports for each worker, so `--scale` would collide; run extra replicas on Kubernetes instead |
134: | Disable a model        | `DELETE /v1/models/{id}` or `PATCH` `status: disabled` (already-queued jobs still run)                                                                                                              |
135: | Add a model or version | [Registry and canary](registry-and-canary.md#adding-a-model-or-a-version)                                                                                                                           |
136: | Run a canary           | [Registry and canary](registry-and-canary.md#running-a-canary)                                                                                                                                      |
137: | Change the schema      | Edit `db/init.sql`, add an **idempotent** file in `migrations/`, apply it by hand to existing databases, and copy the SQL into `k8s/postgres/init-configmap.yaml`                                   |
138: | Rotate `JWT_SECRET`    | Restart the gateway with the new value. Existing JWTs stop working; API keys are unaffected                                                                                                         |
139: | Revoke an API key      | `UPDATE api_keys SET revoked = TRUE WHERE …` in SQL (no API)                                                                                                                                        |
140: | Grant admin            | SQL, see [Getting started](getting-started.md#becoming-an-admin)                                                                                                                                    |
141: | Back up                | Postgres only (users, keys, registry). Redis contents are transient and not durable in Kubernetes                                                                                                   |
142: 
143: Deploy order, as recorded in the Phase 2 documentation: workers accept both the old (payload inside the stream entry) and the current (separate payload key) job format, so **deploy workers before the gateway**, wait for the queue to drain, then deploy the gateway. Rolling the gateway back is safe.
144: 
145: ## Troubleshooting
146: 
147: ```mermaid
148: flowchart TD
149:     A["Job stays queued"] --> B{"Is a worker running whose WORKER_NAME<br/>equals the model's worker_name?"}
150:     B -->|"no"| C["Start it, or correct worker_name in the registry.<br/>Jobs for it are silently skipped by other workers"]
151:     B -->|"yes"| D{"Do worker logs show the maintenance line<br/>about every 30 s?"}
152:     D -->|"no"| E["Consumer process is down. The container should restart<br/>if any process exits; check logs and memory limits"]
153:     D -->|"yes"| F{"XPENDING idle for minutes?"}
154:     F -->|"yes"| G["A consumer is stuck or died. Entries are reclaimed<br/>after RECLAIM_IDLE_MS"]
155:     F -->|"no"| H["Backlog, or the job already expired.<br/>Status lasts 1 h, then 404"]
156: ```
157: 
158: | Symptom                                                              | Likely cause and action                                                                                                                                                                        |
159: | -------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
160: | `GET /v1/models` returns 500, log shows `KeyError: 'canary_percent'` | The database predates migration 004. Apply `migrations/004_canary_percent_and_constraints.sql`                                                                                                 |
161: | Model list is missing the seeded models                              | `init.sql` did not run: it only runs on an empty Postgres volume                                                                                                                               |
162: | Upload returns **500**                                               | Redis is full (`noeviction`) or unreachable. Check `redis-cli INFO memory`. `/ready` can still say ready                                                                                       |
163: | Upload returns 413                                                   | Larger than `MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES`                                                                                                                                                                 |
164: | Upload returns 429                                                   | The key's `rate_limit_per_min` for the current 60 s window. There is no `Retry-After`; wait up to a minute                                                                                     |
165: | `503 Model is currently disabled`                                    | `status` is `disabled`, or you pinned a disabled version                                                                                                                                       |
166: | `404 Unknown model` for an existing model                            | Its only active version is not `v1` and you did not pin it, or the only version is disabled and not `v1`; see [Registry and canary](registry-and-canary.md#how-a-model-version-is-chosen-rest) |
167: | `404 Job not found or expired`                                       | Expired (about 1 to 2 h), wrong job ID, or the job belongs to another user                                                                                                                     |
168: | Job `failed` with `Invalid image data` or `Invalid audio data`       | Permanent input error; no retries were attempted                                                                                                                                               |
169: | Job `failed` after about 14 s of waiting                             | A transient error was retried 3 times; the `error` field has the last message                                                                                                                  |
170: | Jobs complete but ClickHouse has no rows                             | `CLICKHOUSE_HOST` unset, DDL not applied, or the back-off window; look for `metric insert failed`                                                                                              |
171: | Canary receives no traffic                                           | `canary_percent` is set on the wrong row (it is read from the highest version), the model has fewer than two active versions, or you are using the WebSocket path or `?version=`               |
172: | WebSocket closes 4401 / 4404 / 4503 / 1009                           | Bad or missing key / unknown model or version / disabled model / message above `MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES`                                                                                              |
173: | WebSocket error text includes `for url 'http://…'`                   | The worker returned an HTTP error; the message is the raw exception. Fix the worker                                                                                                            |
174: | Worker container restarts repeatedly                                 | One of its three processes exited; check logs. On Kubernetes an out-of-memory kill is likely if the limit is below about 2.5 GiB for OCR                                                       |
